<a id="pm-s1"></a>
## 1. Abstract and result contract

> For every sufficiently large prime <i>q</i>, we construct a finite intersecting $`(q+1)`$-partite $`(q+1)`$-uniform hypergraph with covering number $`q+1`$ and exactly $`q+1`$ nonisolated vertices in each part. This disproves Ryser's covering conjecture, even for intersecting hypergraphs with equally sized parts.

- **C01** (counterexample, [source](#stmt-thm-main)): There exists $q_0$ such that, for every prime $q\ge q_0$, there is a finite intersecting $(q+1)$-partite $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph $H$ with $$\|V_1\|=\cdots=\|V_{q+1}\|=q+1, \qquad \tau(H)=q+1.$$ Every vertex belongs to an edge.
  - Explanation: The construction attains the largest possible covering number for an intersecting uniform hypergraph while keeping every part balanced and every vertex nonisolated. Its prime threshold is not numerical.
- **C02** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-cor-tree-cover)): For every sufficiently large prime $q$, there is a finite complete graph with a $(q+1)$-edge-coloring whose vertices require exactly $q+1$ monochromatic trees to cover. The same minimum holds for connected monochromatic subgraphs.
  - Explanation: Transferring hyperedges to vertices of a complete graph yields the same obstruction for monochromatic tree covers and connected monochromatic subgraph covers.
- **C03** (construction, [source](#stmt-prop-deterministic-reduction)): Let $q\geq4$ be a prime power. Suppose a compatible collection of valid tuples in $\mathbb F_q^2$ satisfies occupancy, concentration of core covers, and the local condition with parameters $0<\rho<1$ and $B\geq0$. If $$\rho q>2(1+B), \label{eq:det-size-condition}$$ then there is an intersecting $(q+1)$-partite $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph with exactly $q+1$ nonisolated vertices in each part and cover number $q+1$. Its edges are in bijection with the set $X$ in <a href="#eq:det-core" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:det-core">[eq:det-core]</a>.
  - Explanation: A specified finite geometric configuration yields the hypergraph only after validity, compatibility, occupancy, core-cover concentration, the local obstruction, and the strict size condition have been established. The implication itself works over prime powers.
- **C04** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-auto-remark-1)): For the multiplicity bound $B=\lceil\log q\rceil$ used in the probabilistic construction, it is enough to require explicitly $q\geq4$ and $\rho q>2(1+\lceil\log q\rceil)$. Every fixed $\rho>0$ satisfies this inequality for all sufficiently large $q$. Primality is not used in the deterministic reduction; any restriction to prime orders must come from establishing the finite configuration properties.
  - Explanation: The logarithmic multiplicity bound eventually satisfies the size inequality for every fixed positive occupancy parameter. Primality belongs to the existence argument rather than the deterministic implication.
- **C05** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-thm-deletion)): Fix $k\ge1$ and $K\ge0$. For each $q$, let $\mathsf Q_q$ be a probability law on the same sets as $\mathsf U_{q,k}$ such that $$\mathsf Q_q(D)\le e^{Kq}\mathsf U_{q,k}(D) \quad\text{for every }D.$$ Then the following assertions hold as $q\to\infty$.* 1. *The empirical probability measure $q^{-2}\sum_{x\in A}\delta_{i_D(x)}$ converges in probability, in total variation, to the Poisson distribution with mean $k$.* 2. *Write $\mu=e^{-k}$. For every fixed $\lambda,\eta>0$, the probability that at least $\eta q$ lines $\ell\in\mathcal L\setminus D$ satisfy $$\left\|\frac{\|\ell\cap R_D\|}{q}-\mu\right\|\ge\lambda$$ tends to zero.* 3. *For every fixed $\delta>0$, the probability that there is a set $C\subseteq\mathcal L\setminus D$ with $\|C\|\le q$ which covers $R_D$ and leaves at least $\delta q^2$ points of $A$ uncovered tends to zero.
  - Explanation: The asymptotic deletion conclusions survive pointwise exponential domination of the uniform deletion law. They concern multiplicity, line occupancy, and covers of the surviving core.
- **C06** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-profile-counting)): For each $q$, let $\mathscr A_q$ be a deterministic class of pairs $(D,C)$ as above. Assume there is $a>0$ such that every pair in every class has either $m=0$ or $m\ge aq$. Suppose every subsequential limit <a href="#eq:profile-limits" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:profile-limits">[eq:profile-limits]</a>, along objects in the classes with $q\to\infty$, satisfies $$\label{eq:visible-excess} \nu_D(\text{$D$-visible profiles})+ \nu_C(\text{$C$-visible profiles})>c.$$ There is $b>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $q$, $$\mathsf U_{q,k}\{D:\text{some }C\text{ has }(D,C)\in\mathscr A_q\} \le \exp(-bq\log q).$$ The constants may depend on $k,a$ and the sequence of classes.
  - Explanation: A strict excess of visible limiting line profiles yields a counting saving strong enough to absorb both witness enumeration and the change of measure. The positive witness-size restriction is part of the hypothesis.
- **C07** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-prime-stability)): Let $\zeta>0$ and put $\delta_\zeta=\min\{\zeta/4,1/100\}$. If $q$ is prime and $q\ge\max\{100,4/\zeta\}$, then any set of at most $q$ distinct affine lines missing fewer than $\delta_\zeta q^2$ affine points has at least $(1-\zeta)q$ members through a common projective point. A point at infinity means that these members are parallel.
  - Explanation: Prime-plane stability converts a sufficiently dense affine cover into a large projective pencil, including the possibility of parallel lines. The explicit size and missing-point conditions are required.
- **C08** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-cor-core-pencil)): Assume the laws of Theorem <a href="#thm:deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:deletion">5</a>, and let $q$ tend to infinity through primes. For each fixed $\zeta>0$, with probability tending to one, every set $C\subseteq\mathcal L\setminus D$ of at most $q$ lines covering $R_D$ has at least $(1-\zeta)q$ members through a common projective point.
  - Explanation: Robust deletion and prime-plane stability force every sufficiently small core cover to concentrate in a projective pencil with high probability.
- **C09** (bound, [source](#stmt-prop-candidate-graph)): There are constants $c_*>0$ and $C_*<\infty$ such that, with probability $1-o(1)$, the following assertions hold simultaneously, with $\beta=1/8$ and $c_0=1/5$:* 1. *$c_*q\le \|Q_g\|\le q$ for every direction $g$.* 2. *Every vertex has degree at most $C_*q$, and has at most $q^\beta$ neighbors in each slot.* 3. *For every $v$ there is a set $J(v)$ of at most $q^\beta$ vertices such that $\|N(v)\cap N(w)\|\le q^\beta$ for $w\notin J(v)$. Every member of $J(v)$ is adjacent to $v$ or is in its slot.* 4. *At most $\lceil\log q\rceil$ constituents pass through any affine point, counting multiplicity. Every line other than one of the $M_g^1$ has at least $c_0q$ points outside $\bigcup_gM_g^1$.* *Consequently the event that these assertions hold and that $M_g^0\ne M_g^1$ for every $g$ has probability $e^{-1}+o(1)$.
  - Explanation: Independent constituent sampling supplies linearly many retained candidates and controls their conflict graph, exceptional codegrees, constituent multiplicity, and initial line occupancy. Constituent distinctness is imposed as a separate event.
- **C10** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pattern-moments)): Let $Z=\sum_a I_a$ count labelled patterns in independent colour variables, where $I_a$ is the indicator of pattern $a$. For each label $a$, let $C_a$ be its deterministic set of tested colours, of uniformly bounded size, so that $I_a$ is measurable in those variables. A bounded set $K_0$ of colours may already have fixed values; all overlap tests below concern the supports $C_a\setminus K_0$.* 1. *If, after fixing any further bounded set of colours, the conditional expected total is $O(1)$ uniformly, then $\mathbb EZ^s=O_s(1)$ for every fixed positive integer $s$.* 2. *Suppose instead that the conditional expected total is $O(q)$, and the conditional expected count of patterns using any of the further exposed colours is $O(1)$. Overlap here excludes the initially fixed colours. Then $$\mathbb E\|Z-\mathbb EZ\|^{2s}=O_s(q^s).$$ All expectations are conditional on the initially fixed colours, if any.
  - Explanation: Uniform conditional pattern counts under bounded colour exposure control moments. The centered estimate additionally requires bounded overlap contributions.
- **C11** (algorithm, [source](#stmt-sel-selection)): Fix constants $c_*,C_*>0$ and $0<c_0\le1$, and put $\beta=1/8$. For each sufficiently large integer $q$, let a finite graph have its vertex set partitioned into $n=2(q+1)$ slots $Q_i$, with no edge within a slot. Write $N(v)$ for the neighborhood of a vertex. Suppose that $$\begin{aligned} c_*q\le \|Q_i\|\le q,&\qquad \|N(v)\|\le C_*q, \qquad \|N(v)\cap Q_i\|\le q^\beta,\label{sel:graph-bounds}\\ \|J(v)\|\le q^\beta,&\qquad \|N(v)\cap N(w)\|\le q^\beta\quad(w\notin J(v)),\label{sel:exceptional} \end{aligned}$$ where every $w\in J(v)$ is either adjacent to $v$ or in its slot. There are constants $K_*,\rho>0$, depending only on $c_*,C_*,c_0$, and a random procedure which either fails or returns an independent transversal $(V_i)_{i\in I_0}$, where $I_0$ is the set of slots and $V_i\in Q_i$. If $\mathcal A$ denotes its event of returning a transversal, then $$\label{sel:success} \mathbb P(\mathcal A)=1-o(1).$$ For any $s$ distinct slots $i_1,\ldots,i_s$ and any prescribed vertices $v_j\in Q_{i_j}$, $$\label{sel:cylinder} \mathbb P\bigl(\mathcal A, V_{i_j}=v_j\ (1\le j\le s)\bigr) \le (K_*/q)^s.$$* *Suppose also that the slots are indexed by $(g,k)$, with two slots for each direction $g$ of an affine plane of order $q$. Associate to each vertex in slot $(g,k)$ a line of direction $g$, injectively within that slot. For every affine line $\ell$, let $T_\ell\subseteq\ell$ be a fixed set of at least $c_0q$ points. From $T_\ell$ remove every point on a selected line different from $\ell$, and call the remaining set $T_\ell^{\rm fin}$. Then the same procedure satisfies $$\label{sel:occupancy-conclusion} \mathbb P\bigl(\mathcal A, \min_\ell \|T_\ell^{\rm fin}\|<\rho q\bigr) =o(1).$$ The errors in <a href="#sel:success" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:success">[sel:success]</a> and <a href="#sel:occupancy-conclusion" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:occupancy-conclusion">[sel:occupancy-conclusion]</a> are uniform in all the stated input data. By the injectivity within a slot, <a href="#sel:cylinder" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="sel:cylinder">[sel:cylinder]</a> also holds for prescribed candidate lines.
  - Explanation: Sequential selection with equalizing thinning and conditional tail completion returns an independent transversal with bounded joint cylinder probabilities. Under the geometric hypotheses it also preserves positive occupancy on every affine line.
- **C12** (bound, [source](#stmt-sel-tail-cylinder)): Let a graph be partitioned into slots of sizes $L_i$, with no within-slot edges. Let $L_{\min}=\min_iL_i$ and let its maximum degree be $\Delta$. If $L_{\min}\ge3$ and $\Delta/L_{\min}\le1/8$, independent uniform choices from the slots have positive probability of being an independent transversal. Under the law conditioned on that event, a cylinder prescribing values in a set $J$ of $s$ slots has probability at most $$\label{sel:tail-cylinder-bound} e^{s/2}\prod_{i\in J}L_i^{-1}.$$ An impossible prescription has probability zero.
  - Explanation: A sufficiently sparse graph of slots has positive independent-transversal probability. Conditioning on independence controls the probability of each prescribed cylinder; impossible prescriptions remain impossible.
- **C13** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-sel-deletion-domination)): Let $D$ consist of the selected split lines and the lines $M_g^1$ in the preceding experiment. Conditional on $\mathcal A$, the law of $D$ is dominated pointwise by $\exp(O(q))$ times the uniform law on sets containing three distinct lines in each direction.
  - Explanation: The deletion set produced by successful selection is pointwise dominated by an exponential factor times the uniform law with the required distinct lines in every direction.
- **C14** (conditional_result, [source](#stmt-prop-pencil-local)): In the independent offset experiment with the selection procedure above, $$\mathbb P\bigl(\mathcal A\text{ and the local condition fails at some } p\in A\bigr)=o(1).$$ Here the local condition, required whenever $b(p)\ge3$, is the prohibition on covers of $F(p)$ by $Z(p)$ and at most $b(p)-1$ additional sets stated in Section <a href="#sec:deterministic-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deterministic-construction">2</a>.
  - Explanation: The probability of violating the finite local covering obstruction anywhere in the affine plane tends to zero jointly with successful selection.
- **C15** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pencil-reuse)): For a fixed affine point $p$, with $\kappa$ the reuse count of the selected tuples through $p$, $$\mathbb P(\mathcal A,\ b(p)\ge2,\ \kappa\ge3)=O(q^{-3}).$$
  - Explanation: At a fixed affine point, repeated generator directions in selected tuples impose a polynomial probability cost, with successful return retained in the event.
- **C16** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pencil-small-reuse)): Fix $p\in A$, $3\le b\le B$, an equality pattern with $\kappa=0$ or $1$, an eligible direction assignment, and the bits. Under the aligned law, the probability of an admissible bad cover is at most $$b^{O(1)}(2B/q)^{3-\kappa}.$$ The polynomial exponent and implicit constant are absolute.
  - Explanation: With little generator reuse, an admissible bad cover forces further incidence constraints under the aligned law. The estimate is joint with admissibility and does not condition point variables on a bad cover.
- **C17** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-pencil-two-reuses)): Fix $p\in A$, $3\le b\le B$, an equality pattern with $\kappa=2$, and the bits. Summed over eligible direction assignments, the probabilities of alignment and an admissible bad cover are at most $$(q+1)^{5b-2}q^{-4b}\,O(b^3/q),\qquad 3\le b\le B.$$
  - Explanation: With the remaining reuse pattern, averaging over eligible directions supplies a further incidence saving. The bound concerns the sum over direction assignments rather than every assignment separately.
- **C18** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-affine-second-moment)): Let $q$ be a prime power, let $U\subseteq\mathbb F_q^2$, and let $\mathcal L$ be the set of all affine lines. Then $$\begin{aligned} \sum_{\ell\in\mathcal L}\|U\cap\ell\|&=(q+1)\|U\|,\\ \sum_{\ell\in\mathcal L}\|U\cap\ell\|^2&=\|U\|^2+q\|U\|,\\ \sum_{\ell\in\mathcal L}\left(\|U\cap\ell\|-\frac{\|U\|}{q}\right)^2 &=q\|U\|-\frac{\|U\|^2}{q}\le\frac{q^3}{4}. \end{aligned}$$ For each direction $d$, define $$V_d=\frac1q\sum_{\ell\parallel d} \left(\|U\cap\ell\|-\frac{\|U\|}{q}\right)^2.$$ Then $\sum_d V_d=\|U\|-\|U\|^2/q^2\le \|U\|\le q^2$.
  - Explanation: Point and ordered-pair incidence counting gives exact affine-line moments and an exact global variance identity over every finite field. The directional variances obey the corresponding summed identity.
- **C19** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-bounded-drift-concentration)): Let $(X_t)_{t=0}^T$ be a real process adapted to a filtration $(\mathcal F_t)_{t=0}^T$, where $T\ge1$ is an integer. Suppose $L>0$ and $\|X_{t+1}-X_t\|\le L$ almost surely for $0\le t<T$. Put $d_t=\mathbb E[X_{t+1}-X_t\mid\mathcal F_t]$. For every $u>0$ and each fixed integer $0\le t\le T$, $$\mathbb P\left(\left\|X_t-X_0-\sum_{j<t}d_j\right\|\ge u\right) \le2\exp\left(-\frac{u^2}{8TL^2}\right).$$
  - Explanation: A bounded-increment adapted process concentrates after subtracting its predictable drift at each fixed time. A maximum over times requires an additional argument.
- **C20** (bound, [source](#stmt-lem-independent-trial-jumps)): Let $Y$ be an integrable real function of independent trials $\xi_1,\ldots,\xi_T$. Suppose changing only trial $i$ changes $Y$ by at most $c_i$, where $c_i\ge0$. The Doob martingale obtained by revealing these trials in order has its $i$th increment bounded in absolute value by $c_i$, and its conditional variance bounded by $c_i^2$. In particular, if $T\ge1$ and $c_i\le L$ for a constant $L>0$, then for every $u>0$, $$\mathbb P(\|Y-\mathbb EY\|\ge u)\le 2\exp\left(-\frac{u^2}{8TL^2}\right).$$
  - Explanation: Independence and bounded coordinate changes control the jumps of the exposure martingale and its conditional variances, yielding the stated concentration bound.

For every fixed $\\lambda,\\eta>0$, the probability that at least $\\eta q$ lines $\\ell\\in\\mathcal L\\setminus D$ satisfy $$\\left|\\frac{|\\ell\\cap R_D|}{q}-\\mu\\right|\\ge\\lambda$$ tends to zero.*\n\n3.  *For every fixed $\\delta>0$, the probability that there is a set $C\\subseteq\\mathcal L\\setminus D$ with $|C|\\le q$ which covers $R_D$ and leaves at least $\\delta q^2$ points of $A$ uncovered tends to zero.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.1"
        }
      ],

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-profile-counting",
        "exact_quote": "For each $q$, let $\\mathscr A_q$ be a deterministic class of pairs $(D,C)$ as above. Assume there is $a>0$ such that every pair in every class has either $m=0$ or $m\\ge aq$. Suppose every subsequential limit <a href=\"#eq:profile-limits\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:profile-limits\">[eq:profile-limits]</a>, along objects in the classes with $q\\to\\infty$, satisfies $$\\label{eq:visible-excess}\n \\nu_D(\\text{$D$-visible profiles})+\n \\nu_C(\\text{$C$-visible profiles})>c.$$ There is $b>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $q$, $$\\mathsf U_{q,k}\\{D:\\text{some }C\\text{ has }(D,C)\\in\\mathscr A_q\\}\n \\le \\exp(-bq\\log q).$$ The constants may depend on $k,a$ and the sequence of classes.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.1"
      },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-prime-stability",
        "exact_quote": "Let $\\zeta>0$ and put $\\delta_\\zeta=\\min\\{\\zeta/4,1/100\\}$. If $q$ is prime and $q\\ge\\max\\{100,4/\\zeta\\}$, then any set of at most $q$ distinct affine lines missing fewer than $\\delta_\\zeta q^2$ affine points has at least $(1-\\zeta)q$ members through a common projective point. A point at infinity means that these members are parallel.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.1"
      },
      "evidence": [

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-prop-candidate-graph",
        "exact_quote": "There are constants $c_*>0$ and $C_*<\\infty$ such that, with probability $1-o(1)$, the following assertions hold simultaneously, with $\\beta=1/8$ and $c_0=1/5$:*\n\n1.  *$c_*q\\le |Q_g|\\le q$ for every direction $g$.*\n\n2.  *Every vertex has degree at most $C_*q$, and has at most $q^\\beta$ neighbors in each slot.*\n\n3.  *For every $v$ there is a set $J(v)$ of at most $q^\\beta$ vertices such that $|N(v)\\cap N(w)|\\le q^\\beta$ for $w\\notin J(v)$. Every member of $J(v)$ is adjacent to $v$ or is in its slot.*\n\n4.  *At most $\\lceil\\log q\\rceil$ constituents pass through any affine point, counting multiplicity. Every line other than one of the $M_g^1$ has at least $c_0q$ points outside $\\bigcup_gM_g^1$.*\n\n*Consequently the event that these assertions hold and that $M_g^0\\ne M_g^1$ for every $g$ has probability $e^{-1}+o(1)$.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-prop-candidate-graph",
          "exact_quote": "There are constants $c_*>0$ and $C_*<\\infty$ such that, with probability $1-o(1)$, the following assertions hold simultaneously, with $\\beta=1/8$ and $c_0=1/5$:*\n\n1.  *$c_*q\\le |Q_g|\\le q$ for every direction $g$.*\n\n2.  *Every vertex has degree at most $C_*q$, and has at most $q^\\beta$ neighbors in each slot.*\n\n3.  *For every $v$ there is a set $J(v)$ of at most $q^\\beta$ vertices such that $|N(v)\\cap N(w)|\\le q^\\beta$ for $w\\notin J(v)$. Every member of $J(v)$ is adjacent to $v$ or is in its slot.*\n\n4.  *At most $\\lceil\\log q\\rceil$ constituents pass through any affine point, counting multiplicity. Every line other than one of the $M_g^1$ has at least $c_0q$ points outside $\\bigcup_gM_g^1$.*\n\n*Consequently the event that these assertions hold and that $M_g^0\\ne M_g^1$ for every $g$ has probability $e^{-1}+o(1)$.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.1"
        }
      ],
      "exact_statement": "There are constants $c_*>0$ and $C_*<\\infty$ such that, with probability $1-o(1)$, the following assertions hold simultaneously, with $\\beta=1/8$ and $c_0=1/5$:*\n\n1.  *$c_*q\\le |Q_g|\\le q$ for every direction $g$.*\n\n2.  *Every vertex has degree at most $C_*q$, and has at most $q^\\beta$ neighbors in each slot.*\n\n3.  *For every $v$ there is a set $J(v)$ of at most $q^\\beta$ vertices such that $|N(v)\\cap N(w)|\\le q^\\beta$ for $w\\notin J(v)$.     {
      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-lem-pattern-moments",
        "exact_quote": "Let $Z=\\sum_a I_a$ count labelled patterns in independent colour variables, where $I_a$ is the indicator of pattern $a$. For each label $a$, let $C_a$ be its deterministic set of tested colours, of uniformly bounded size, so that $I_a$ is measurable in those variables. A bounded set $K_0$ of colours may already have fixed values; all overlap tests below concern the supports $C_a\\setminus K_0$.*\n\n1.  *If, after fixing any further bounded set of colours, the conditional expected total is $O(1)$ uniformly, then $\\mathbb EZ^s=O_s(1)$ for every fixed positive integer $s$.*\n\n2.  *Suppose instead that the conditional expected total is $O(q)$, and the conditional expected count of patterns using any of the further exposed colours is $O(1)$. Overlap here excludes the initially fixed colours. Then $$\\mathbb E|Z-\\mathbb EZ|^{2s}=O_s(q^s).$$ All expectations are conditional on the initially fixed colours, if any.",
        "source_id": "PM-162.1"
      },
      "evidence": [
        {
          "anchor": "stmt-lem-pattern-moments",
          "exact_quote": "Let $Z=\\sum_a I_a$ count labelled patterns in independent colour variables, where $I_a$ is the indicator of pattern $a$. Overlap here excludes the initially fixed colours. Then $$\\mathbb E|Z-\\mathbb EZ|^{2s}=O_s(q^s).$$ All expectations are conditional on the initially fixed colours, if any.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.1"
        }
      ],

          "exact_quote": "Let $Z=\\sum_a I_a$ count labelled patterns in independent colour variables, where $I_a$ is the indicator of pattern $a$. For each label $a$, let $C_a$ be its deterministic set of tested colours, of uniformly bounded size, so that $I_a$ is measurable in those variables. A bounded set $K_0$ of colours may already have fixed values; all overlap tests below concern the supports $C_a\\setminus K_0$.*\n\n1.  *If, after fixing any further bounded set of colours, the conditional expected total is $O(1)$ uniformly, then $\\mathbb EZ^s=O_s(1)$ for every fixed positive integer $s$.*\n\n2.  *Suppose instead that the conditional expected total is $O(q)$, and the conditional expected count of patterns using any of the further exposed colours is $O(1)$. Overlap here excludes the initially fixed colours. Then $$\\mathbb E|Z-\\mathbb EZ|^{2s}=O_s(q^s).$$ All expectations are conditional on the initially fixed colours, if any.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.1"
        }
      ],
      "statement_anchor": "stmt-lem-pattern-moments"
    },

      "conclusion": {
        "anchor": "stmt-sel-selection",
        "exact_quote": "Fix constants $c_*,C_*>0$ and $0<c_0\\le1$, and put $\\beta=1/8$. For each sufficiently large integer $q$, let a finite graph have its vertex set partitioned into $n=2(q+1)$ slots $Q_i$, with no edge within a slot. Write $N(v)$ for the neighborhood of a vertex. Suppose that $$\\begin{aligned}\n c_*q\\le |Q_i|\\le q,&\\qquad |N(v)|\\le C_*q,\n \\qquad |N(v)\\cap Q_i|\\le q^\\beta,\\label{sel:graph-bounds}\\\\\n |J(v)|\\le q^\\beta,&\\qquad\n |N(v)\\cap N(w)|\\le q^\\beta\\quad(w\\notin J(v)),\\label{sel:exceptional}\n\\end{aligned}$$ where every $w\\in J(v)$ is either adjacent to $v$ or in its slot. There are constants $K_*,\\rho>0$, depending only on $c_*,C_*,c_0$, and a random procedure which either fails or returns an independent transversal $(V_i)_{i\\in I_0}$, where $I_0$ is the set of slots and $V_i\\in Q_i$. If $\\mathcal A$ denotes its event of returning a transversal, then $$\\label{sel:success}\n \\mathbb P(\\mathcal A)=1-o(1).$$ For any $s$ distinct slots $i_1,\\ldots,i_s$ and any prescribed vertices $v_j\\in Q_{i_j}$, $$\\label{sel:cylinder}\n \\mathbb P\\bigl(\\mathcal A, V_{i_j}=v_j\\ (1\\le j\\le s)\\bigr)\n \\le (K_*/q)^s.$$",
        "source_id": "PM-162.1"
      },

          "exact_quote": "Fix $p\\in A$, $3\\le b\\le B$, an equality pattern with $\\kappa=2$, and the bits. Summed over eligible direction assignments, the probabilities of alignment and an admissible bad cover are at most $$(q+1)^{5b-2}q^{-4b}\\,O(b^3/q),\\qquad 3\\le b\\le B.$$",

        }

        "exact_quote": "Let $q$ be a prime power, let $U\\subseteq\\mathbb F_q^2$, and let $\\mathcal L$ be the set of all affine lines. <a id="pm-s2"></a>
## 2. Definitions, validity and bounds

- **Vertex cover** ([source](#eq:ryser)): A vertex cover of a finite hypergraph $H$ is a set of vertices meeting every edge.
- **Covering number** ([source](#eq:ryser)): Its minimum possible size is the *covering number* $\tau(H)$.
- **Matching number** ([source](#eq:ryser)): The *matching number* $\nu(H)$ is the maximum number of pairwise disjoint edges.
- **Partite uniform hypergraph** ([source](#eq:ryser)): For an integer $r\ge2$, an $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraph has disjoint vertex parts $V_1,\ldots,V_r$, and each edge contains exactly one vertex from each part.
- **Balance** ([source](#eq:ryser)): Here *balance* refers to equal sizes of the vertex parts.
- **Intersecting hypergraph** ([source](#eq:ryser)): A hypergraph with at least one edge is *intersecting* if every two distinct edges meet.
- **Edge coloring** ([source](#pm-h-3)): An $r$-edge-coloring assigns one of $r$ colors to each edge of a graph.
- **Monochromatic tree cover** ([source](#pm-h-3)): A monochromatic tree cover is a family of monochromatic trees whose vertex sets cover the graph; overlaps and singleton trees are allowed.
- **Merged pair and constituent** ([source](#pm-h-8)): For every direction $g$, choose two distinct lines $M_g^0,M_g^1\in\mathcal L_g$. Call their union the merged pair in direction $g$, and call either of its lines a constituent.
- **Tuple** ([source](#pm-h-8)): A tuple in direction $g$ consists of four distinct directions $d_0,d_1,d_2,d_3\in\mathcal G\setminus\{g\}$ and four bits $\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\sigma_3\in\{0,1\}$.
- **Split line and designated set** ([source](#pm-h-8)): We call $S$ its split line and write $F_S=\{u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3\}$.
- **Deletion core and retained sets** ([source](#eq:det-core)): Let $\mathcal S$ be the set of chosen split lines, and define $$\begin{split} D&=\mathcal S\cup\{M_g^1:g\in\mathcal G\},\\ R_0&=A\setminus\bigcup_{\ell\in D}\ell, \qquad R=A\setminus\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal S}S,\\ X&=R\cup\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal S}F_S. \end{split} \label{eq:det-core}$$
- **Partner coverage** ([source](#eq:det-partner-set)): For $p\in A$, define $$\mathcal S(p)=\{S\in\mathcal S:p\in S\},\qquad b(p)=\|\mathcal S(p)\|,\qquad F(p)=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal S(p)}F_S,$$ and $$Z(p)=F(p)\cap \bigcup_{\substack{d\in\mathcal G,\ s\in\{0,1\}\\p\in M_d^s}} M_d^{1-s}. \label{eq:det-partner-set}$$
- **Uniform deletion law** ([source](#sec:deletion)): For $q\ge k$, let $\mathsf U_{q,k}$ be the uniform law on sets $D\subseteq\mathcal L$ containing exactly $k$ lines in each direction.
- **Total variation distance** ([source](#sec:deletion)): For probability measures on a countable set, total variation distance is one half of the sum of the absolute coordinate differences.
- **Reuse count** ([source](#pm-h-44)): Define the reuse count $$\kappa=4b-\|\Gamma\|=\sum_{d\in\Gamma}(t_d-1).$$
- **Directional variance** ([source](#stmt-lem-affine-second-moment)): For each direction $d$, define $$V_d=\frac1q\sum_{\ell\parallel d} \left(\|U\cap\ell\|-\frac{\|U\|}{q}\right)^2.$$

            "anchor": "stmt-lem-pattern-moments",
            "exact_quote": "Let $Z=\\sum_a I_a$ count labelled patterns in independent colour variables, where $I_a$ is the indicator of pattern $a$. For each label $a$, let $C_a$ be its deterministic set of tested colours, of uniformly bounded size, so that $I_a$ is measurable in those variables. A bounded set $K_0$ of colours may already have fixed values; all overlap tests below concern the supports $C_a\\setminus K_0$.*\n\n1.  *If, after fixing any further bounded set of colours, the conditional expected total is $O(1)$ uniformly, then $\\mathbb EZ^s=O_s(1)$ for every fixed positive integer $s$.*\n\n2.  *Suppose instead that the conditional expected total is $O(q)$, and the conditional expected count of patterns using any of the further exposed colours is $O(1)$. Overlap here excludes the initially fixed colours. Then $$\\mathbb E|Z-\\mathbb EZ|^{2s}=O_s(q^s).$$ All expectations are conditional on the initially fixed colours, if any.",
            "source_id": "PM-162.1"
          }
        ],
        "parameter_scope": "A fixed moment order may be chosen sufficiently large for each desired inverse-power tail bound.",
        "quantifiers": [

            "exact_quote": "Let a graph be partitioned into slots of sizes $L_i$, with no within-slot edges. Let $L_{\\min}=\\min_iL_i$ and let its maximum degree be $\\Delta$. The Doob martingale obtained by revealing these trials in order has its $i$th increment bounded in absolute value by $c_i$, and its conditional variance bounded by $c_i^2$. In particular, if $T\\ge1$ and $c_i\\le L$ for a constant $L>0$, then for every $u>0$, $$\\mathbb P(|Y-\\mathbb EY|\\ge u)\\le\n 2\\exp\\left(-\\frac{u^2}{8TL^2}\\right).$$",

<a id="pm-s3"></a>
## 3. Proof roadmap

- **R01** [claim](#stmt-lem-affine-second-moment) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-affine-second-moment): Count points and ordered pairs on affine lines. -> Exact incidence and directional variance identities.
- **R02** [claim](#stmt-lem-bounded-drift-concentration) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-bounded-drift-concentration): Center predictable drift and bound conditional exponential moments. -> A fixed-time concentration inequality.
- **R03** [claim](#stmt-lem-independent-trial-jumps) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-independent-trial-jumps): Couple future independent trials when a coordinate is changed. -> Bounded exposure jumps and the corresponding concentration inequality.
- **R04** [claim](#stmt-lem-profile-counting) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-profile-counting): Interleave exposures to detect visible profiles, then count marked sequences. -> A uniform forbidden-profile counting estimate.
- **R05** [claim](#stmt-thm-deletion) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-profile-counting-2): Apply profile detection to multiplicity, occupancy, and failed almost-covers. -> Robust asymptotic deletion estimates.
- **R06** [claim](#stmt-lem-prime-stability) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-prime-stability): Projectively dualize the affine line cover and apply prime-plane stability. -> A large projective pencil.
- **R07** [claim](#stmt-cor-core-pencil) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-core-pencil): Combine almost-covering with prime-plane stability. -> Concentration of every small core cover.
- **R08** [claim](#stmt-lem-pattern-moments) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pattern-moments): Expand pattern moments and organize overlaps by connected components. -> Moment bounds for pattern counts.
- **R09** [claim](#stmt-prop-candidate-graph) / [proof](#sec:candidates): Count aligned tuples, exclude invalid witnesses, and translate incidence estimates into graph bounds. -> Candidate graph and initial occupancy bounds.
- **R10** [claim](#stmt-sel-tail-cylinder) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-tail-cylinder): Use local-lemma avoidance conditioning and bound cylinder inflation. -> Positive tail-transversal probability and a conditional cylinder bound.
- **R11** [claim](#stmt-sel-selection) / [proof](#sec:selection): Select slots and vertices, equalize survival by thinning, stop and censor concentration tracks, and complete the sparse tail. -> A successful compatible selection with occupancy and joint cylinder bounds.
- **R12** [claim](#stmt-sel-deletion-domination) / [proof](#proof-stmt-sel-deletion-domination): Count assignments of deletion lines to constituent and split roles. -> Pointwise exponential domination of the returned deletion law.
- **R13** [claim](#stmt-lem-pencil-reuse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pencil-reuse): Extract bounded generator-reuse witnesses and expose their offsets successively. -> A probability saving for large reuse.
- **R14** [claim](#stmt-lem-pencil-small-reuse) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pencil-small-reuse): Mark surplus cover incidences and extract acyclic variable dependencies. -> Further incidence savings for an admissible bad cover.
- **R15** [claim](#stmt-lem-pencil-two-reuses) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pencil-two-reuses): Average over direction tokens and reveal a required direction only after its geometric constraint is known. -> An averaged incidence saving for the remaining reuse pattern.
- **R16** [claim](#stmt-prop-pencil-local) / [proof](#pm-h-48): Sum the reuse and bad-cover bounds over pencils and affine points. -> The local finite obstruction holds with asymptotically vanishing joint failure.
- **R17** [claim](#stmt-prop-deterministic-reduction) / [proof](#pm-h-9): Build balanced partitions, force a dominant pencil, and exhaust its residual covering budget. -> The required finite intersecting balanced hypergraph.
- **R18** [claim](#stmt-thm-main) / [proof](#proof-stmt-lem-pencil-two-reuses-2): Intersect successful selection with occupancy, core-cover concentration and local obstruction events. -> Existence of the balanced hypergraph counterexample.
- **R19** [claim](#stmt-cor-tree-cover) / [proof](#proof-stmt-cor-tree-cover): Make hyperedges graph vertices and color each pair by a shared part. -> The monochromatic tree-cover counterexample.

Suppose every subsequential limit <a href=\"#eq:profile-limits\" data-reference-type=\"eqref\" data-reference=\"eq:profile-limits\">[eq:profile-limits]</a>, along objects in the classes with $q\\to\\infty$, satisfies $$\\label{eq:visible-excess}\n \\nu_D(\\text{$D$-visible profiles})+\n \\nu_C(\\text{$C$-visible profiles})>c.$$ There is $b>0$ such that, for all sufficiently large $q$, $$\\mathsf U_{q,k}\\{D:\\text{some }C\\text{ has }(D,C)\\in\\mathscr A_q\\}\n \\le \\exp(-bq\\log q).$$ The constants may depend on $k,a$ and the sequence of classes.",

For each $q$, let $\\mathsf Q_q$ be a probability law on the same sets as $\\mathsf U_{q,k}$ such that $$\\mathsf Q_q(D)\\le e^{Kq}\\mathsf U_{q,k}(D)\n \\quad\\text{for every }D.$$ Then the following assertions hold as $q\\to\\infty$.*\n\n1.  *The empirical probability measure $q^{-2}\\sum_{x\\in A}\\delta_{i_D(x)}$ converges in probability, in total variation, to the Poisson distribution with mean $k$.*\n\n2.  *Write $\\mu=e^{-k}$. For every fixed $\\lambda,\\eta>0$, the probability that at least $\\eta q$ lines $\\ell\\in\\mathcal L\\setminus D$ satisfy $$\\left|\\frac{|\\ell\\cap R_D|}{q}-\\mu\\right|\\ge\\lambda$$ tends to zero.*\n\n3.  *For every fixed $\\delta>0$, the probability that there is a set $C\\subseteq\\mathcal L\\setminus D$ with $|C|\\le q$ which covers $R_D$ and leaves at least $\\delta q^2$ points of $A$ uncovered tends to zero.",
          "source_id": "PM-162.1"
        }

<a id="pm-s4"></a>
## 4. Engineering Triptych

### Panel 1 - epistemic contract

- **I-incidence**: The affine-line intersection sums and their centered second moment are exact for every point subset.
- **I-prime**: The main balanced counterexamples exist for every sufficiently large prime order; the threshold is not numerical.
- **I-conditional**: The deterministic reduction requires valid compatible tuples, occupancy, concentration of core covers, the local condition, and the strict size inequality.

### Panel 2 - negative knowledge

- **A-threshold**: Treat the existence proof as a ready finite counterexample generator. -> An unspecified threshold supplies no admitted concrete field order or successful configuration.
- **A-field**: Use integer modular line enumeration at every prime-power order. -> Modular arithmetic at a composite order need not model a finite field.
- **A-transfer**: Use the affine incidence identity as certification of the balanced hypergraph counterexamples. -> The identity supplies no valid split tuples or cover obstruction.

### Panel 3 - reference implementation and oracle

Applicability: `constructive`. The elementary affine incidence lemma gives finite exact sums for a specified point subset. Its implementation does not certify the asymptotic hypergraph existence theorem.

```python
# INPUTS: JSON object q, masks; each mask encodes a subset by bit x*q+y.
# PRECONDITIONS: q is one of the primes 2,3,5,7; masks form a nonempty list of at most 512 distinct integers in [0,2**(q*q)).
# OUTPUTS: JSON rows [first moment,second moment,variance numerator,variance denominator,directional numerator,directional denominator].
# TIME: O(len(masks)*q**4) exact arithmetic operations.
# SPACE: O(q**3+len(masks)) stored point tuples, integers and rational numbers.
# ARITHMETIC: prime-field modular integers and exact python-flint fmpz/fmpq arithmetic.
# FAILURE: malformed inputs raise ValueError; unsupported prime powers are rejected.
import json, sys
from flint import fmpz, fmpq
obj = json.load(sys.stdin)
q = obj['q']
masks = obj['masks']
if type(q) is not int or q not in (2,3,5,7):
    raise ValueError('unsupported field')
if not isinstance(masks,list) or len(masks)>512 or not masks or any(type(m) is not int or not 0<=m<2**(q*q) for m in masks) or len(set(masks))!=len(masks):
    raise ValueError('invalid subsets')
lines = [[(x,y) for x in range(q) for y in range(q) if (y-a*x)%q==b] for a in range(q) for b in range(q)]
lines += [[(x,y) for y in range(q)] for x in range(q)]
rows = []
for mask in masks:
    points = {(x,y) for x in range(q) for y in range(q) if mask & (1<<(x*q+y))}
    counts = [sum(p in points for p in line) for line in lines]
    first = sum(fmpz(c) for c in counts)
    second = sum(fmpz(c)*c for c in counts)
    mean = fmpq(len(points),q)
    variance = sum((fmpq(c)-mean)**2 for c in counts)
    directional = variance / q
    rows.append([int(first),int(second),int(variance.numerator),int(variance.denominator),int(directional.numerator),int(directional.denominator)])
print(json.dumps(rows,separators=(',',':')))
```

    "oracle_code": "import json, sys\nfrom fractions import Fraction\ntry:\n    request = json.load(open(sys.argv[1]))\n    order = request['q']\n    answer = json.load(open(sys.argv[2]))\n    expected = json.loads(sys.argv[3])\n    truth = []\n    for subset in request['masks']:\n        size = subset.bit_count()\n        total = (order+1)*size\n        pairs = size*(size-1)+(order+1)*size\n        fluctuation = Fraction(order*size)-Fraction(size*size,order)\n        by_direction = Fraction(size)-Fraction(size*size,order*order)\n        assert 0 <= fluctuation <= Fraction(order**3,4)\n        assert 0 <= by_direction <= size <= order*order\n        truth.append([total,pairs,fluctuation.numerator,fluctuation.denominator,by_direction.numerator,by_direction.denominator])\n    if answer != truth or expected != truth:\n        sys.exit(6)\nexcept (ValueError,KeyError,TypeError,AssertionError):\n    sys.exit(8)\n",
    "output_contract": "Exact first and second intersection sums, centered variance, and summed directional variance as rational numerator-denominator pairs.",
    "reason": "The elementary affine incidence lemma gives finite exact sums for a specified point subset. Its implementation does not certify the asymptotic hypergraph existence theorem.",
    "reference_code": "# INPUTS: JSON object q, masks; each mask encodes a subset by bit x*q+y.\n# PRECONDITIONS: q is one of the primes 2,3,5,7; masks form a nonempty list of at most 512 distinct integers in [0,2**(q*q)).\n# OUTPUTS: JSON rows [first moment,second moment,variance numerator,variance denominator,directional numerator,directional denominator].\n# TIME: O(len(masks)*q**4) exact arithmetic operations.\n# SPACE: O(q**3+len(masks)) stored point tuples, integers and rational numbers.\n# ARITHMETIC: prime-field modular integers and exact python-flint fmpz/fmpq arithmetic.\n# FAILURE: malformed inputs raise ValueError; unsupported prime powers are rejected.\nimport json, sys\nfrom flint import fmpz, fmpq\nobj = json.load(sys.stdin)\nq = obj['q']\nmasks = obj['masks']\nif type(q) is not int or q not in (2,3,5,7):\n    raise ValueError('unsupported field')\nif not isinstance(masks,list) or len(masks)>512 or not masks or any(type(m) is not int or not 0<=m<2**(q*q) for m in masks) or len(set(masks))!=len(masks):\n    raise ValueError('invalid subsets')\nlines = [[(x,y) for x in range(q) for y in range(q) if (y-a*x)%q==b] for a in range(q) for b in range(q)]\nlines += [[(x,y) for y in range(q)] for x in range(q)]\nrows = []\nfor mask in masks:\n    points = {(x,y) for x in range(q) for y in range(q) if mask & (1<<(x*q+y))}\n    counts = [sum(p in points for p in line) for line in lines]\n    first = sum(fmpz(c) for c in counts)\n    second = sum(fmpz(c)*c for c in counts)\n    mean = fmpq(len(points),q)\n    variance = sum((fmpq(c)-mean)**2 for c in counts)\n    directional = variance / q\n    rows.append([int(first),int(second),int(variance.numerator),int(variance.denominator),int(directional.numerator),int(directional.denominator)])\nprint(json.dumps(rows,separators=(',',':')))\n",
    "test_vectors": [

<a id="pm-s5"></a>
## 5. Domain engineering contracts

| domain | applies | strength | maturity | basis | why |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| software | True | weak | inspirational | inferred | The cover counterexamples can inform audits of software assumptions that impose the conjectured covering bound on all intersecting multipartite hypergraphs. This is an inferred testing use; an executable counterexample is not supplied. |
| games_simulation | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: the geometric sampling experiment establishes mathematical existence and supplies no game mechanics or simulation performance contract. |
| tools | True | weak | adaptable | inferred | The exact affine incidence identities can inform diagnostic checks of finite-plane computations. This tool use is inferred from the identities, without a supplied software implementation. |
| algorithms | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The paper states a random independent-transversal procedure with conflict deletion, equal-survival thinning and conditioned tail completion. Its asymptotic guarantees support adapting selection analyses under the stated graph hypotheses; efficient finite-size execution is not established. |
| data_structures | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The candidate graph organizes valid geometric tuples into slots and represents incompatibility by edges. Adapting this representation separates tuple validity from pairwise compatibility; storage bounds and a software data structure are absent. |
| hardware_accelerators | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: the finite geometric construction contains no accelerator architecture, hardware arithmetic contract or hardware performance result. |
| crypto_security | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: finite fields and random offsets serve incidence and covering arguments; the paper provides no cryptographic primitive or security reduction. |
| machine_learning | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: the concentration arguments concern specified adapted processes rather than learning models or training guarantees. |
| optimisation_operations_research | True | moderate | adaptable | inferred | The covering objective and the counterexample class inform analysis of multipartite hypergraph cover bounds. The result refutes a proposed universal bound but supplies neither an optimisation algorithm nor a concrete instance at a specified order. |
| distributed_systems | False | none | not_applicable | inferred | ABSENT: random slot selection and geometric compatibility do not specify communication, distributed coordination or a fault model. |
| other (finite_geometry_and_extremal_combinatorics) | True | strong | adaptable | stated | Finite geometry and extremal combinatorics are the direct setting: a conditional deterministic reduction over prime-power fields combines with probabilistic existence and prime-plane stability to produce balanced intersecting hypergraphs with the asserted covering number. |

<a id="pm-s6"></a>
## 6. Applicability limits and search

- **L01** (counterexample_scope): The main counterexamples exist for every sufficiently large prime order; the assertion is existential within the specified intersecting, partite and uniform class. Blocked use: ['Generalizing existence to every field order or to every balanced hypergraph.']
- **L02** (unknown): The threshold for the main construction is not numerical. Blocked use: ['Selecting a concrete order and claiming the main theorem guarantees a successful instance at that order.']
- **L03** (hypothesis): The deterministic reduction applies over a prime power only after validity, compatibility, occupancy, core-cover concentration, the local condition and the strict size inequality have been established. Blocked use: ['Treating a finite geometric configuration as a counterexample before checking all reduction hypotheses.']
- **L04** (edge_case): The finite configuration requirements do not themselves establish existence of such a configuration. Blocked use: ['Using the deterministic specification alone as an existence proof.']
- **L05** (hypothesis): Primality is needed to establish core-cover concentration through the prime-plane stability input, although the deterministic reduction does not use primality. Blocked use: ['Replacing the main prime regime with an unrestricted prime-power regime.']
- **L06** (counterexample_scope): The tree-cover consequence concerns complete graphs with the stated edge-coloring and sufficiently large prime order. Blocked use: ['Applying this existence result as a universal covering guarantee for arbitrary graphs or colorings.']
- **L07** (approximation): High probability is an asymptotic assertion along the orders under discussion. Auxiliary constants remain fixed before the order varies. Blocked use: ['Interpreting asymptotic success as a numerical reliability guarantee at a chosen finite order.']
- **L08** (hypothesis): The robust deletion estimates require pointwise domination of the deletion law by the uniform law with a fixed exponential factor. Blocked use: ['Applying the deletion conclusions to an arbitrary biased selection law without proving domination.']
- **L09** (hypothesis): The prescribed-choice estimate includes successful return jointly with the prescriptions; it does not assert independence of selected tuples. Blocked use: ['Multiplying selected-tuple probabilities as though the returned tuples were independent.']
- **L10** (edge_case): The bounded-drift concentration bound is stated at a fixed time; a bound for a maximum over times requires an additional argument. Blocked use: ['Using the fixed-time estimate unchanged as a uniform bound over all times.']
- **L11** (hypothesis): The independent-trial concentration estimate requires independent trials and a bound on the effect of changing each trial. Blocked use: ['Applying the estimate to dependent trials or an unbounded coordinate change without a separate justification.']
- **L12** (formal_scope): The affine second-moment identity is a finite incidence statement. Checking it on finite inputs supplies evidence for that component alone. Blocked use: ['Claiming that finite incidence checks certify the asymptotic construction or its existence threshold.']

> - [A counterexample to Ryser's covering conjecture](../../preprints/A-Counterexample-to-Rysers-Covering-Conjecture-September-23-2026/paper.pdf)
>
> ## Scope
>
> Ryser's covering conjecture predicts that an intersecting $r$-partite hypergraph has a vertex cover of size at most $r-1$. The formalization proves that every sufficiently large prime $q$ has a finite intersecting $(q+1)$-partite, $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph with covering number $q+1$ and exactly $q+1$ nonisolated vertices in each part. Thus the conjecture fails even with equal part sizes. The prime threshold is existential.
>
> Ryser's covering conjecture predicts $\tau\le(r-1)\nu$ for an $r$-partite hypergraph, where $\tau$ and $\nu$ are its covering and matching numbers. The formalized constructions give finite intersecting $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraphs with $\nu=1$ and $\tau=r$, contradicting the bound. The ranks have the form $r=p^n+1$: one fixed prime $p\equiv2\pmod3$ works for all sufficiently large prime degrees $n$, and a second result covers every sufficiently large such prime $p$ and every sufficiently large odd $n$, with the degree threshold allowed to depend on $p$. Infinitely many ranks occur. Equal part sizes and numerical thresholds are not asserted.

```lean
import Mathlib

namespace OAI

namespace Balanced
open scoped _root_.Balanced

structure PartiteHypergraph (r n : ℕ) where
  edges : Finset (Fin r → Fin n)

namespace PartiteHypergraph

variable {r n : ℕ}

abbrev Vertex (r n : ℕ) := Fin r × Fin n

def Covers (H : PartiteHypergraph r n) (C : Finset (Vertex r n)) : Prop :=
  ∀ e ∈ H.edges, ∃ i, (i, e i) ∈ C

def Intersecting (H : PartiteHypergraph r n) : Prop :=
  H.edges.Nonempty ∧ ∀ e ∈ H.edges, ∀ f ∈ H.edges, ∃ i, e i = f i

def Nonisolated (H : PartiteHypergraph r n) : Prop :=
  ∀ i v, ∃ e ∈ H.edges, e i = v

def HasCoverNumber (H : PartiteHypergraph r n) (k : ℕ) : Prop :=
  (∃ C : Finset (Vertex r n), H.Covers C ∧ C.card = k) ∧
    ∀ C : Finset (Vertex r n), H.Covers C → k ≤ C.card

end PartiteHypergraph

def BalancedCounterexample (q : ℕ) : Prop :=
  ∃ H : PartiteHypergraph (q + 1) (q + 1),
    H.Intersecting ∧ H.Nonisolated ∧ H.HasCoverNumber (q + 1)

def MainResult : Prop :=
  ∃ q₀ : ℕ, ∀ q : ℕ, q₀ ≤ q → q.Prime → BalancedCounterexample q

theorem main_result : MainResult := by
  sorry

end Balanced

end OAI
```

<a id="pm-s7"></a>
## 7. Complete mathematical paper


For every sufficiently large prime <i>q</i>, we construct a finite intersecting $`(q+1)`$-partite $`(q+1)`$-uniform hypergraph with covering number $`q+1`$ and exactly $`q+1`$ nonisolated vertices in each part. This disproves Ryser's covering conjecture, even for intersecting hypergraphs with equally sized parts.

<a id="pm-h-2"></a>
### Introduction

<a id="eq:ryser"></a>
A vertex cover of a finite hypergraph $H$ is a set of vertices meeting every edge. Its minimum possible size is the *covering number* $\tau(H)$. The *matching number* $\nu(H)$ is the maximum number of pairwise disjoint edges. For an integer $r\ge2$, an $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraph has disjoint vertex parts $V_1,\ldots,V_r$, and each edge contains exactly one vertex from each part. Here *balance* refers to equal sizes of the vertex parts. Ryser’s covering conjecture proposes that every such hypergraph satisfies $$\label{eq:ryser}
 \tau(H)\le (r-1)\nu(H).$$ A hypergraph with at least one edge is *intersecting* if every two distinct edges meet. In this case $\nu(H)=1$, and the proposed bound is $r-1$. A cover may use vertices from any of the parts, so making each individual part large does not by itself obstruct a small cover.

For $r=2$, inequality <a href="#eq:ryser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ryser">[eq:ryser]</a> is Kőnig’s matching theorem for bipartite graphs. Henderson’s 1971 thesis contains an equivalent formulation of the general conjecture (Henderson 1971) <a id="pm-cite-1"></a>[[Henderson1971](#ref-Henderson1971)]; Best and Wanless (Best and Wanless 2018) <a id="pm-cite-2"></a>[[BestWanless2018](#ref-BestWanless2018)] discuss its attribution to Ryser and separate it from his Latin-square transversal conjecture. Aharoni proved the full tripartite case (Aharoni 2001) <a id="pm-cite-3"></a>[[Aharoni2001](#ref-Aharoni2001)]. In the intersecting case, Gyárfás proved the conjecture through rank four and Tuza proved rank five; see Király and Tóthmérész (Király and Tóthmérész 2017) <a id="pm-cite-4"></a>[[KiralyTothmeresz2017](#ref-KiralyTothmeresz2017)] for the historical account. For *linear* intersecting hypergraphs, in which every two distinct edges meet in exactly one vertex, Francetić, Herke, McKay, and Wanless established the conjecture through rank nine (Francetić et al. 2017) <a id="pm-cite-5"></a>[[FranceticHerkeMcKayWanless2017](#ref-FranceticHerkeMcKayWanless2017)]. For each of $r=4,5$, Haxell and Scott proved a bound $\tau(H)\le(r-\varepsilon_r)\nu(H)$ with a positive constant $\varepsilon_r$ (Haxell and Scott 2012) <a id="pm-cite-6"></a>[[HaxellScott2012](#ref-HaxellScott2012)]. More recently, White proved $\tau(H)\le6$ for four-partite four-uniform hypergraphs with $\nu(H)=2$ (White 2026) <a id="pm-cite-7"></a>[[White2026](#ref-White2026)]. These results concern the covering inequality itself. Lovász proposed the stronger assertion that, in every $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraph with at least one edge, deleting $r-1$ vertices can reduce the matching number. Clow, Haxell, and Mohar disproved that assertion at $r=3$ (Clow, Haxell, and Mohar 2026) <a id="pm-cite-8"></a>[[ClowHaxellMohar2025](#ref-ClowHaxellMohar2025)]; Abiad, Garbe, Povill, and Spiegel subsequently gave an infinite family at $r=3$ and examples at $r=4$ (Abiad et al. 2025) <a id="pm-cite-9"></a>[[AbiadGarbePovillSpiegel2025](#ref-AbiadGarbePovillSpiegel2025)]. Those examples do not contradict <a href="#eq:ryser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ryser">[eq:ryser]</a>.

Finite planes explain why the bound $r-1$ is natural. In the affine plane $\mathbb F_q^2$, make one part for each of the $q+1$ directions, with one vertex for each line of that direction. An affine point determines an edge consisting of the labels of the lines through it. Two such edges meet at the label of their joining line. Each parallel class is a cover of size $q$, and fewer than $q$ lines cannot cover $q^2$ points. Thus this hypergraph is intersecting and has covering number $q$. It is the usual truncated-projective-plane example, expressed through affine duality.

Abu-Khazneh, Barát, Pokrovskiy, and Szabó exploit the rigidity of minimum covers in a truncated plane and add a new part and exceptional edges. For every prime power $q\ge3$, their construction yields an intersecting $(q+2)$-partite $(q+2)$-uniform hypergraph with covering number $q+1$ (Abu-Khazneh et al. 2019, Theorem 1.2, Lemma 1.3, Corollary 1.4) <a id="pm-cite-10"></a>[[AbuKhaznehBaratPokrovskiySzabo2019](#ref-AbuKhaznehBaratPokrovskiySzabo2019)]. Haxell and Scott use affine planes to construct intersecting $r$-partite $r$-uniform hypergraphs with covering number at least $r-4$ for every sufficiently large $r$, and at least $r-3$ when $r$ is even (Haxell and Scott 2017, Theorem 6) <a id="pm-cite-11"></a>[[HaxellScott2017](#ref-HaxellScott2017)].

Equal part sizes alone do not force a large covering number: Francetić, Herke, McKay, and Wanless constructed a linear intersecting 13-partite hypergraph with 13 nonisolated vertices in each part, and reported that its covering number is nine by computation (Francetić et al. 2017, sec. 3.1) <a id="pm-cite-12"></a>[[FranceticHerkeMcKayWanless2017](#ref-FranceticHerkeMcKayWanless2017)]. Here we modify the affine-plane example while keeping $q+1$ parts, one for each direction. The modification raises both the covering number and each part size to $q+1$.

<a id="stmt-thm-main"></a>
<div id="thm:main" class="theorem">

**Theorem 1**. *There exists $q_0$ such that, for every prime $q\ge q_0$, there is a finite intersecting $(q+1)$-partite $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph $H$ with $$|V_1|=\cdots=|V_{q+1}|=q+1,
 \qquad \tau(H)=q+1.$$ Every vertex belongs to an edge.*

</div>

Since $\nu(H)=1$, this gives counterexamples to <a href="#eq:ryser" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:ryser">[eq:ryser]</a>. The value $\tau(H)=r$, where $r=q+1$, is the largest possible for an intersecting $r$-uniform hypergraph, since any edge is a cover. The nonisolated vertices in each part also form a cover, so a hypergraph with $\tau(H)=r$ must have at least $r$ such vertices in every part. Our construction attains this minimum simultaneously in all parts.

The argument produces an example for each prime beyond one fixed threshold; that threshold is not numerical. The companion article (OpenAI 2026, Theorem 1.1) <a id="pm-cite-13"></a>[[OpenAIExtension2026](#ref-OpenAIExtension2026)] treats a different range of field orders: for every sufficiently large prime $s\equiv2
\pmod3$, it gives counterexamples of order $q=s^n$ for every sufficiently large odd $n$, with the threshold for $n$ depending on $s$. Its theorem does not assert balance. The proof here is independent of that construction.

<a id="pm-h-3"></a>
#### Monochromatic tree covers

An $r$-edge-coloring assigns one of $r$ colors to each edge of a graph. A monochromatic tree cover is a family of monochromatic trees whose vertex sets cover the graph; overlaps and singleton trees are allowed. Gyárfás’s tree-cover conjecture asks for at most $r-1$ such trees in every $r$-edge-colored complete graph (Milićević 2017, Conjecture 1) <a id="pm-cite-14"></a>[[Milicevic2017](#ref-Milicevic2017)]. Erdős, Gyárfás, and Pyber record the equivalence of this covering formulation with the intersecting case of Ryser’s conjecture (Erdős, Gyárfás, and Pyber 1991, sec. 3, after Theorem 2) <a id="pm-cite-15"></a>[[ErdosGyarfasPyber1991](#ref-ErdosGyarfasPyber1991)]. The standard transfer gives the following consequence of Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>.

<a id="stmt-cor-tree-cover"></a>
<div id="cor:tree-cover" class="corollary">

**Corollary 2**. *For every sufficiently large prime $q$, there is a finite complete graph with a $(q+1)$-edge-coloring whose vertices require exactly $q+1$ monochromatic trees to cover. The same minimum holds for connected monochromatic subgraphs.*

</div>

<details id="proof-stmt-cor-tree-cover"><summary>Proof of stmt-cor-tree-cover</summary>

*Proof.* Put $r=q+1$ and take the hypergraph $H$ from Theorem <a href="#thm:main" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="thm:main">1</a>. Use its edges as the vertices of a complete graph. Color a pair $ef$ by any index $i$ for which $e$ and $f$ have the same vertex in part $V_i$; intersection guarantees such an index. Along a path of color $i$, this vertex of $V_i$ is constant. Thus each connected monochromatic subgraph corresponds to a hypergraph vertex lying in all the hyperedges represented by its vertices; for a singleton choose any vertex of the corresponding hyperedge. A cover by $t$ such subgraphs would give a vertex cover of $H$ of size at most $t$, so $t\ge\tau(H)=r$. Conversely, the $r$ monochromatic stars centered at any fixed graph vertex cover the complete graph, allowing singleton stars for unused colors. ◻

</details>

<a id="pm-h-4"></a>
#### Balance, intersection, and the cover obstruction

The affine-plane example starts with $q$ blocks in each parallel class. We replace those line partitions by partitions with $q+1$ blocks, keeping one vertex part for each direction. In a given direction, merge two parallel lines into one block, and split two other parallel lines into two blocks each. The count becomes $$(q-4)+1+4=q+1.$$ Each split line keeps just four designated points. Writing them in a cyclic order as $u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3$, its blocks are the opposite pairs $\{u_0,u_2\}$ and $\{u_1,u_3\}$. All points outside the chosen split lines are retained as well. The choices ensure that designated points lie on no other chosen split line, so these rules really give partitions of one common set of retained points. The blocks label vertices and the retained points label edges.

The splitting operation creates the first difficulty: two points on a split line may now have different labels in that direction. We arrange four merged pairs in four distinct other directions so that each consecutive pair $u_{i-1},u_i$ lies in a common merged block. These are exactly the pairs separated by the opposite-pair partition. Thus every pair of retained points still shares a block somewhere, and the resulting hypergraph remains intersecting. Figure <a href="#fig:cycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cycle">1</a> shows the rule; Section <a href="#sec:deterministic-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deterministic-construction">2</a> states its precise incidence and compatibility requirements.

The harder difficulty is to rule out a cover using labels from several parts. Temporarily remove all split lines and one constituent of each merged pair. On the surviving *core*, every remaining block is represented by a single ordinary line. Hence a cover with at most $q$ labels gives a cover of the core by at most $q$ lines. We prove that almost all those lines belong to one *projective pencil*: they pass through one affine point, or are all parallel if the common projective point is at infinity.

Every relevant line also retains a positive proportion of its points. This occupancy forces a hypothetical cover to select every nonsplit member of its pencil. Only a small budget of labels remains for the four-point sets on split lines through the pencil’s center. Compatibility handles the parallel case and pencils containing at most two split lines. For larger pencils, a separate incidence count shows that the remaining budget is insufficient. The deterministic result in Section <a href="#sec:deterministic-construction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deterministic-construction">2</a> makes these requirements explicit before we construct data satisfying them.

<a id="pm-h-5"></a>
#### Producing the geometric data

The probabilistic argument has to preserve its estimates through several choices. First sample two independent constituent lines in each direction. They generate many candidate four-point tuples on different split lines. An incompatibility graph records choices that cannot coexist. There are two slots per direction, each carrying the candidates for one split line. Choosing one candidate in every slot without an incompatible pair is an *independent transversal* of this graph.

Section <a href="#sec:candidates" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:candidates">4</a> bounds the sizes and degrees of this graph, including the exceptional pairs that can have large common neighborhoods. Section <a href="#sec:selection" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:selection">5</a> then constructs a random independent transversal while maintaining line occupancy. The procedure first makes random choices and adds thinning to equalize one-step survival; it completes the last slots using the local lemma. Partial random selection followed by local-lemma completion also appears in the locally sparse transversal argument of Loh and Sudakov (Loh and Sudakov 2007, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-16"></a>[[LohSudakov2007](#ref-LohSudakov2007)]. Here we need an additional quantitative output: prescribing the choices in $s$ slots, jointly with successful return, has probability at most $(K_*/q)^s$ for a fixed constant $K_*$. This bound controls later incidence counts without assuming that selected tuples are independent.

The core estimate in Section <a href="#sec:deletion" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:deletion">3</a> is designed for the law of these selected lines. Under uniform deletion of a fixed number of lines per direction, with probability tending to one every small core cover also covers almost all of the whole plane. The key counting lemma bounds forbidden profile classes by $\exp(-\Omega(q\log q))$. After the preliminary density and occupancy restrictions are imposed, this saving pays for both the possible covers and the $\exp(O(q))$ change of measure introduced by selection; the resulting almost-cover conclusion holds with probability tending to one under the changed law. Random line constructions and the counting of small covers through collinear subsets have precedents in Erdős and Lovász (Erdős and Lovász 1975, sec. 4) <a id="pm-cite-17"></a>[[ErdosLovasz1975](#ref-ErdosLovasz1975)]; Kahn subsequently developed the random-line approach further (Kahn 1992) <a id="pm-cite-18"></a>[[Kahn1992](#ref-Kahn1992)]. The profile argument here supplies the change-of-measure control needed for our selection law. We then apply the prime-plane stability theorem of Szőnyi and Weiner (Szőnyi and Weiner 2012) <a id="pm-cite-19"></a>[[SzonyiWeiner2012](#ref-SzonyiWeiner2012)], also stated by Héger and Nagy (Héger and Nagy 2017) <a id="pm-cite-20"></a>[[HegerNagy2017](#ref-HegerNagy2017)], to turn an almost-cover into a large pencil. This is the step that requires $q$ to be prime.

Finally, Section <a href="#sec:pencils" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:pencils">6</a> combines the prescribed-choice bound with the original independent constituent offsets. It counts repeated generator directions and the additional incidences that a small local cover would require. Section <a href="#sec:assembly" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:assembly">7</a> intersects the resulting successful events and applies the deterministic construction. Appendix <a href="#sec:elementary" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="sec:elementary">8</a> supplies three elementary estimates in a form independent of the construction; these are also the precise inputs used by the companion article.

<a id="pm-h-6"></a>
###### Conventions.

All logarithms are natural. An event holds with high probability if its probability tends to one along the orders under discussion. Constants in $O(\cdot)$ may depend on parameters fixed before $q$ varies. Every auxiliary constant is fixed independently of $q$; the final threshold may depend on those constants.

<a id="sec:deterministic-construction"></a>
### From modified line partitions to a hypergraph

The geometric data needed for the theorem have two jobs: preserve the intersection of point-edges after lines are split, and rule out every cover using at most $q$ block labels. We formulate both requirements here and prove that they suffice. The later probabilistic sections will produce the data; all arguments in this section are deterministic and work over any finite field.

Let $q\geq4$ be a prime power, let $A=\mathbb F_q^2$, and let $\mathcal G$ be the set of its $q+1$ directions. For $g\in\mathcal G$, write $\mathcal L_g$ for the $q$ affine lines of direction $g$, and put $\mathcal L=\bigcup_g\mathcal L_g$. The affine line of direction $g$ through $p\in A$ is denoted by $p+g$. We also use the projective completion $\operatorname{PG}(2,q)$. Thus a common projective point of affine lines means either an ordinary point of concurrence or the point at infinity of a parallel class.

<a id="pm-h-8"></a>
#### Merged pairs and four-point split lines

For every direction $g$, choose two distinct lines $M_g^0,M_g^1\in\mathcal L_g$. Call their union the merged pair in direction $g$, and call either of its lines a constituent. Write $$\mathcal M=\{M_g^s:g\in\mathcal G,\ s\in\{0,1\}\}.$$ If $S\in\mathcal L_g\setminus\mathcal M$, define a simple graph on the points of $S$: two distinct points are adjacent if they are the two intersections of $S$ with $M_d^0,M_d^1$ for some $d\ne g$. Several directions may give the same adjacency; its multiplicity is irrelevant.

A tuple in direction $g$ consists of four distinct directions $d_0,d_1,d_2,d_3\in\mathcal G\setminus\{g\}$ and four bits $\sigma_0,\sigma_1,\sigma_2,\sigma_3\in\{0,1\}$. Indices on the tuple are read modulo $4$. Define <a id="eq:det-tuple-points"></a>$$u_i=M_{d_i}^{\sigma_i}\cap M_{d_{i+1}}^{1-\sigma_{i+1}}.
 \label{eq:det-tuple-points}$$ The intersections exist uniquely because consecutive generator directions are different. Call the tuple valid if the following conditions hold:

1.  Its four points are distinct and lie on one line $S\in\mathcal L_g\setminus\mathcal M$.

2.  Neither $\{u_0,u_2\}$ nor $\{u_1,u_3\}$ is an adjacency on $S$.

3.  Every point of $S\setminus\{u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3\}$ is adjacent to at most one of $u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3$.

We call $S$ its split line and write $F_S=\{u_0,u_1,u_2,u_3\}$. Each consecutive pair $\{u_{i-1},u_i\}$ is adjacent: the two points lie on the two different constituents of direction $d_i$. Consequently the two opposite pairs partition $F_S$, and every pair crossing that partition shares a merged pair.

Figure <a href="#fig:cycle" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="fig:cycle">1</a> records the partition and the shared merged blocks. The conditions on opposite pairs and external points will also control covers centered at a point of $S$.

We need two split lines in every direction. Index these requirements by slots $(g,s)$, where $g\in\mathcal G$ and $s\in\{1,2\}$, and choose a valid tuple in each slot. Write $S_g^s$ for its split line. The choices are *compatible* if the following conditions hold for any two different slots:

1.  Neither chosen split line contains a designated point of the other tuple.

2.  The two sets of generator directions have intersection of size at most one.

3.  If the slots have the same target direction, no merged pair contains at least two designated points of one tuple and at least one designated point of the other.

The first condition makes all chosen split lines distinct and makes their designated point sets pairwise disjoint. In particular, there are exactly two split lines in each direction, both different from its two constituents. The second condition will be relevant to constructing such data; the deterministic implication below uses the first and third conditions.

<figure id="fig:cycle">

<figcaption>The merge and split operation gives $q + 1$ blocks per direction. Below, the four points on one split line form two opposite-pair blocks, shown by filled and open points. An arc labelled $d_{i}$ means that its endpoints lie on different constituents of the merged pair in direction $d_{i}$, and hence share its block label. The arcs record this relation; they are not affine lines. The diagram is schematic.</figcaption>
</figure>

<a id="pm-figsrc-fig-cycle"></a>

   - Vertical arrows lead from "$q - 4$ other lines" to "$q - 4$ blocks", from "$M_g^0, M_g^1$" to "$1$ merged block", and from "$S_g^1, S_g^2$" to "$4$ pair blocks".
   - Bottom row labelled "new blocks:": contains three rounded boxes containing "$q - 4$ blocks", "$1$ merged block", and "$4$ pair blocks".

<a id="eq:det-core"></a>
<a id="eq:det-split-intersection"></a>
Let $\mathcal S$ be the set of chosen split lines, and define $$\begin{split}
 D&=\mathcal S\cup\{M_g^1:g\in\mathcal G\},\\
 R_0&=A\setminus\bigcup_{\ell\in D}\ell,
 \qquad R=A\setminus\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal S}S,\\
 X&=R\cup\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal S}F_S.
 \end{split}
 \label{eq:det-core}$$ There are exactly three lines of $D$ in each direction. Compatibility gives $$S\cap X=F_S\qquad(S\in\mathcal S).
 \label{eq:det-split-intersection}$$ Indeed, $R$ contains no point of $S$, and no other designated point set meets $S$.

The inclusion $R_0\subseteq R$ gives two retained sets with different purposes. On $R_0$, one constituent of each merged block has disappeared, so a block cover becomes an ordinary line cover. The larger set $R$ supplies points on the removed constituents when we later force a cover to choose their merged blocks. The four-point sets supply the final obstruction.

The following three properties express exactly what these sets must do. Fix $0<\rho<1$ and an integer $B\geq0$.

Occupancy.  
At most $B$ constituent lines pass through any affine point. Every $\ell\in\mathcal L\setminus D$ satisfies $|\ell\cap R_0|\geq\rho q$, and every $M_g^1$ satisfies $|M_g^1\cap R|\geq\rho q$.

Concentration of core covers.  
If $C\subseteq\mathcal L\setminus D$, $|C|\leq q$, and the lines of $C$ cover $R_0$, then at least $(1-\rho/4)q$ lines of $C$ pass through a common projective point.

<a id="eq:det-partner-set"></a>
The local condition.  
For $p\in A$, define $$\mathcal S(p)=\{S\in\mathcal S:p\in S\},\qquad
  b(p)=|\mathcal S(p)|,\qquad F(p)=\bigcup_{S\in\mathcal S(p)}F_S,$$ and $$Z(p)=F(p)\cap
 \bigcup_{\substack{d\in\mathcal G,\ s\in\{0,1\}\\p\in M_d^s}}
 M_d^{1-s}.
 \label{eq:det-partner-set}$$ Whenever $b(p)\geq3$, the set $F(p)$ cannot be covered by $Z(p)$ and at most $b(p)-1$ additional sets of the following types: sets of at most two points; affine lines outside $\mathcal S(p)\cup\mathcal M$; and merged pairs $M_d^0\cup M_d^1$, with no merged pair used more than once.

All these are properties of a specified finite configuration. In particular, none asserts that such a configuration exists.

<a id="stmt-prop-deterministic-reduction"></a>
<div id="prop:deterministic-reduction" class="proposition">

<a id="eq:det-size-condition"></a>
**Proposition 3** (Deterministic reduction). *Let $q\geq4$ be a prime power. Suppose a compatible collection of valid tuples in $\mathbb F_q^2$ satisfies occupancy, concentration of core covers, and the local condition with parameters $0<\rho<1$ and $B\geq0$. If $$\rho q>2(1+B),
 \label{eq:det-size-condition}$$ then there is an intersecting $(q+1)$-partite $(q+1)$-uniform hypergraph with exactly $q+1$ nonisolated vertices in each part and cover number $q+1$. Its edges are in bijection with the set $X$ in <a href="#eq:det-core" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:det-core">[eq:det-core]</a>.*

</div>

<a id="pm-h-9"></a>
#### Partitions and intersecting edges

We prove Proposition <a href="#prop:deterministic-reduction" data-reference-type="ref" data-reference="prop:deterministic-reduction">3</a> in three stages: construct the balanced partitions, reduce a small cover to one pencil, and test the designated points against its remaining budget.

For each $g\in\mathcal G$, partition $X$ into these blocks:

- $\ell\cap X$ for every regular line $\ell\in\mathcal L_g$, where “regular” excludes the two split lines and the two constituents in that direction;

- $(M_g^0\cup M_g^1)\cap X$;

- $\{u_0,u_2\}$ and $\{u_1,u_3\}$ for each of its two split lines, using that line’s tuple ordering.

Distinct lines of $\mathcal L_g$ are disjoint and partition $A$, so <a href="#eq:det-split-intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:det-split-intersection">[eq:det-split-intersection]</a> proves that these blocks partition $X$. There are $(q-4)+1+4=q+1$ of them. The split blocks have two points. Every regular block is nonempty by the lower bound on $|\ell\cap R_0|$, and the merged block is nonempty by the same lower bound for $M_g^0$.

Let $V_g$ consist of distinct labels for these blocks; choose disjoint label sets for different directions. A point $x\in X$ defines an edge $e_x$ containing, for each $g$, the unique label of the block containing $x$. Thus every edge contains exactly one vertex from each of the $q+1$ parts. Every label occurs in an edge because its block is nonempty.

For different $x,y\in X$, let $\ell$ be their joining line and let $g$ be its direction. If $\ell$ is not a split line, then $x,y$ are in the same regular or merged block of $V_g$. If $\ell$ is a split line, both points are designated by <a href="#eq:det-split-intersection" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:det-split-intersection">[eq:det-split-intersection]</a>. They either belong to the same opposite-pair block or form consecutive positions in its four-cycle. In the latter case they share the merged block of the corresponding generator direction. Hence $e_x\cap e_y\ne\varnothing$.

Repeated edges would not affect the cover argument, but they do not occur here. If $e_x=e_y$ for different $x,y$, then in every direction $h$ different from the direction of their joining line they lie on different $h$-lines. Their only possible common block in $V_h$ is therefore the merged block. The point $x$ would lie on a constituent in each of these $q$ directions, contradicting $B<q$, which follows from <a href="#eq:det-size-condition" data-reference-type="eqref" data-reference="eq:det-size-condition">[eq:det-size-condition]</a>. Thus $x\mapsto e_x$ is injective.

Selecting labels covers all edges if and only if the corresponding blocks cover $X$. We will show that at least $q+1$ blocks are needed. The reverse bound follows because all labels in any one part cover every edge.

<a id="pm-h-10"></a>
#### Forcing the nonsplit members of a pencil

We have obtained an intersecting hypergraph with the required part sizes and no isolated vertices. Its covering number is the minimum number of blocks covering $X$. We now use $R_0$ to locate any hypothetical small cover, then use occupancy on $R_0$ and $R$ to force its nonsplit blocks.

Suppose that a collection $\mathcal B$ of at most $q$ labels covers $X$. A split block misses $R_0$. On $R_0$, a merged block is exactly $M_g^0\cap R_0$, because $M_g^1\in D$. Associate to each selected regular block its line and to each selected merged block the line $M_g^0$. These representative lines are distinct, belong to $\mathcal L\setminus D$, and cover $R_0$. Concentration of core covers provides a projective point $P$ lying on at least $(1-\rho/4)q$ selected representatives. Write $t$ for the number of selected labels that do not have a representative through $P$, including all selected split labels. Then <a id="eq:det-outside-pencil"></a>$$t\leq\rho q/4.
 \label{eq:det-outside-pencil}$$ We treat separately a parallel pencil and a pencil through an affine point.

<a id="pm-h-11"></a>
###### A point at infinity.

Suppose $P$ is the point at infinity of direction $g$. Every one of the $q-3$ lines of $\mathcal L_g\setminus D$ must be a selected representative. Otherwise an omitted such line has at least $\rho q$ points in $R_0$, none covered by the parallel representatives through $P$, while all remaining representatives cover at most $t$ of them. Split blocks cover none of $R_0$, and $t<\rho q$.

The corresponding $q-3$ selected blocks cover none of the eight designated points on the two split lines of direction $g$. An additional regular block covers at most one point on each of those lines, and a split block has size two. A merged pair of direction $g$ misses both split lines. A merged pair of any other direction has at most two designated points on each split line; if it had at least three altogether, it would have at least two on one and at least one on the other, contrary to the third compatibility condition. Thus every additional block covers at most two of these eight points. At most three labels remain in the cover budget, so a cover is impossible.

<a id="pm-h-12"></a>
###### An affine point.

Now let $P=p\in A$. We first show that for each direction $g$ such that $p+g$ is not a split line, the selected labels must include its regular block or, when $p+g$ is a constituent, its merged block.

If $\ell=p+g\notin D$ and its block is omitted, selected representatives through $p$ cover at most the point $p$ on $\ell$. All other representatives cover at most another $t$ points there.
